with agricultural land. Fled the gentleman oppovite any idea of the condition of agricultural labourers? He(Mr. New) had seen men in Lincolnshire who bed worked for many years on farms (twelve hours a day) at the magnificent salary of 14e. a week. And yet the President of the Board of Agriculture looked to such men for the defence of the Empire. Some landlords had • way of grinding down lesseholders. Directly their hoses expired they had to pay... increase rate for renewal or sacrifice all they bed invested. It would bee great mistake for the Government to advance money to be invented by private individuals, eepeolally in agricultural land which was an exceedingly uncertain thing. How wan it, continued Mr. New, that English wheat mi. 3s. 64. per quarter cheaper then foreign wheat ?

.....„ ... Mr. H E. TIAmTHORNE (C., E. Belfast) : Because foreign wheat is worth more. It is more weal to the bakers. They cannot an, English wheat for some purposes. Mr. New : English wheat Is cheaper not became Colonial wheat is more useful, but because English I Agriculture Ms been crushed almost out of existence owing to the landowners desire to coneerve the country In a narrow sphere. THE CHANCELLOR OF THE Ext if NICER (Mr. P.O. Taylor) raid a weaker attack upon any measure yet introduced in that House he had never beard. The only point raised against the Bill was that in relation to Clause 4. The peculiarity of the Bill was that it oven framed upon the exact and precise basis upon which the imperial Government loaned money at the present time for the purpose of the buying of lend. The hon. member Wen attacking the principles of his cows Govern- ment. If English nip-Molter° was being crushed by the greed of the landlord,as it was alleged to ber,thee it am a remarkable thing that, in the beret few days, a county farmers' association bad congratulated local farmers on the fact that a certain Cheshire landlord had acquired n certain estate, which would make for their benefit. Why wee it that under the two systems under which there could be tenancy at the present time—indi vides] landlordism and tenancy under County Councils—the latest innovation, tenancy under the County Councils had proved an entire failure? In less than two generations,under the system which would he eatahlished by the Bill of that House, instead of the County Council being the owner after the tenant had spent his blood and bones on the land, the oocupier would be the owner and no one would have power to turn him out. It was significant of the *salines. of the Opposition in raising the point about short lessee that they had overlooked the fact that the more a tenant pat into his farm in the way of improvementa during the period of the lame, ',be more compensation he received when he gave up hie farm. Mr. R. ['fence WILLIAMS (S., Aeglemy) agrsed with the Minister of •griculture that the Bill was the antithesia of Socialism. Be did not regard it no en isolated measure but as part of a ootoprehensi ye plan, and he thought the Asti-Socialist League had some- thing to do with it. (Laughter.) The object ass to enlarge the number of property owners. (Hear, bear.) Property owners were at present such an object of attack—he quite admitted it—that they naturally sought to widen end strengthen their defences. The object of the Bill wee to create an army of small property owners to form an outer line of defence, so that the attack on the citadel of individual property and individnallem would be deferred. The Socialists in France had been taunted with their inability to cepture the smell peasant proprietors Why? Because they hadss Mr. Chesterton said,the passion of a peasant for his field it won because of that premien for property that he (the speaker) opposed the Bill. He believed, conscientiously and sincerely,that one form of property —public property ea. the right thine for this country. The reason why the yeomanry had disappeared from our land system was bemuse It Wee found that agriculture on a large male was more profitable than smell farms. The rapture wart a curious one for tariff reformers to support, for their ambition was understood to he to make Enelend the workshop of the world. That, how- ever, would be impossible of attainment if they were going to convert the industrial population into peasant proprietor,. Mr. G CHAMBERLAIN (L, Gorton) mid the Bill as it stood would not help the development of land. If 1 landlordism was no comiderate, how wet it that tenants who applied for small holdings had been evicted ? The only solution of the question Wan to he found in I the taxation of land values, which would render it 1 peceseary for landowners to seek the serviCen of wed- cultural labourers to help them to develop their land. I The PRINIE MINISTeR (Mr. W. H. Stenefield) said the I Bill was the sequel ,to the action which the Imperial government was persistently pursuing with regard to the land owning class. In consequence of the attempts to impose excessive taxation on landowner., many of that class had found it would be ruinous to keep their land any longer and farms had thus come Into the market at nominal rents. The object of the Bill was that instead of the land coming into the hands of speculatomthe tenants who had experience of work- ing it, should have the opportunity of acquiring it. He would like ter see a law whereby lend should he taken away from tenants who did not term it. properly. ROYAL COURT THEATRE

" The Merry Widow."

Records are still being broken by "The Merry Widow," which seems as popular to-day as when it was first produced and became the rage of theatre- goers all over England and the Continent. Certainly no more entrancing play with music hae seen the light during the present generation ; its haunting melodies absolutely defy satiety, while the passionate love-duel, the dancing, and the Slavonic setting remain irresistibly fascinating. The second visit of Mr. George Edwardes' Company to the Royal Court Theatre has drawn crowded attendance each evening this w-ek and the general opinion is that the perfor- mance excels even that of last year's company. There have been several changes in the cast, and interest is if anyehing, enhanoed thereby. A new Sonia is always the subject of speculation and pleasant anticipation. The " Merry Widow " title ride is an rich in pcssibili- tie, that every fresh artiste must find ample scope for the outlet of her own particular talents. Of Miss Mary Ridley's "Sonia " we have nothing but the highest praise. Miss Ridley is a very beautiful girl, equipped with all the grace and zemperameutal qualities which are essential to • vivid impersonation of "the Merry Widow." Her singing is obviously the product of careful training, and she has enjoyed a most enthusiastic apprecia- tion of the solos which fall to her lot Equally pleasing too, is Mr. J. Warren Foster in the. companion role of Prince Nail. Blessed with good looks and a distinguished air, Mr. Foster persues the elusive widow with • fervour which ber.omes all the more evident as it is suppressed by the exigencies of that incomparable eituation where two people are violently in lave, and yet prefer to remain studiously aloof through pique. Prince Danilo and Sonia are a captivating pair, and in the great waltz scene earned that generous applauee which is their just due. The humour of the play— and how exquisite much of it is—finds admirable expression through thole quaint ch•ractere, Baron Popoff and Nisch. Mr. H. Sinclair Cotter makes quite the drollest Baron we have seen. Every quip and every gesture is ripe with humour, and the laughter of the audience is spontaneous and prolonged. Nisch is admirably personated by Mr. Harry Emeric, who brings an individuality of hie own to the explession of the many quaint conceits which go to make up the character of this remarkakable valet. Two other companion r(des are those of Natalie end the Vicomte de Jolidon. Much of the more serious vocal work is in their hands and both distinguish themselves. Miss Mabel Hope sang" lam a dutiful wife " and "Take care, take care " with a wealth of expression, while ' Mr. Ripley Evans' pure-toned tenor voice was always beard with great gratification. Erou-Frou's dancing is something to marvel at, and Mitts Katie May should go far in those branches of the fascinating terpeichorean art where the biwrre inns essential. In addition to the ditties of stage manager, Mr. Clifford Leyler manages to he exeeedingly comic as General Novakovitch, the officer with the 'flirtatious wife. Tile chorus is exceedingly strong and the dreams of the numerous ladies who frequent Maxim's are a source of admira- tion and wonder to the feminine portion of the audience. As usual the play in gorgeously mounted, and the scenes are full of splendour. A fine inter- pretation of Franz Lehar's seductive music is given Sec an augmented orchestra under the experienced control of Mr. J. A. E. Malone.

A SIMPLE PRESCRIPTION

SAID TO CURE RHEUMATISM, KIDNEY TROUBLE AND BLADDER TROUBLES.

We know that the readers of this paper appreciate ' good advice, and we are glad. therelore, to be able to publish the following from a prominent physician. It is astonishing to know the prevalence of kidney trouble and other diseases, such as rheumatism, bladder and liver troubles, which are almost invariably calmed by weak kidneys. It is said on good anthoritv that fully one-third of the people in the United Kingdom who have reached the age of maturity are more or less afflicted with kidney trouble in some form. The most dangerous feature of this disease is the fact that many people have it without their know- ledge, and consequently neglect it until it has retched a dangerous stage. The least symptom of kidney troubre, such as back- ache, must be given prompt attention, and a good reliable treatment should lie taken, until every trace of the di-ease has been eliminated. We are glad to be able to give our readers a prescription which we have seldom known to fail in any case. It

conta- ins-no harmful ingredients, is pleasant to take, not expensive, and can be supplied by any good chemist. It is as follows:

_ Tinctme Gentian Compound, one ounce; Syrup Rhubarb, one ounce; Liquid Berkola Compound, one ounce; Syrup of Ginger, five ounces. Take a tettapoonful after each meal, and one at bedtime. . . .

We are glad to publi.h this prescription, as we know it will help our al:holed readers who give it a fair trial. If taken continuously for a few weeks, it will be found to be an almost absolutely certain cure for Rheumatism, Kidney and Bladder trouble, Back- ache, and all allied complaints. . .

LOCAL PARLIAMENT.

TORIES AND THE LAND QUESTION. A Peasant Proprietary Bill.

There was a falling-off in the attendance at the meet- ing of the Parliamentary Debating Society on Tneeday, as compared with the previous week, the subject for diacustoon being a Bill to provide facilities for the sale of land to occupying tenants and to extend the aviitem ol Peasant Proprietary in England end Wales. As the division was not to be taken that evening, many hon. members did not deem it necessary to put in appear- ance. but if the society is to fulfil its purpose more loyal support should be accorded to it. The terms of the measure were published in last week's " Examiner." Briefly, it allows the Board of Agriculture to make advances to tenants to enable them to acquire their farms, the advances to one person not to exceed £7,000. The total advances made by the lioard are not to exceed £10,000.000 without further assent by Parliament. With regard to smaller hold- ings, the Board may acquire lands for the purpose of re-sale in quantities of not less than three acres and not more than 100 acres In extent to any one purchaser. It is provided that the Board may buy lands compulsorily excepting where it shall be shewn that where part of as estate only is purchased the owner will he prejudiced by the intended purchase. tar. C. PLISSTON (President of the Board of Agri- cultnre), in introducing the measure' said that tinder the English system of agriculture the landlord owned the capital and the tenant was the producer of the outcome of the soil. If they examined the system, it practiciAly resolved itself into the some groove as interest and capital applied to industrial undertakings. The great advantage was that the farmer with a small capital could cultivate a larger plot of land than he could if ha were the owner. As a general rule it paid the farmer better to be a tenant, because at the present time more profit was derived front the cultivation of land than from its ownership. There v.as very little friction between landlords and tenants, as the laws were equitable to both and were accepted as such. A disquieting feature of agriculture undoubtedly was the great exodus from the rural districts. The yeomen farmers were a thing of the pest and the small freeholders were now almost ' non-existent. A revival of small ownership was essential because it brought out the best qualities of a man. His had an interest in the land that he cultivated and it was only human nature for him to pot more pains and attention into it-s farming than if he were timply • tenant. The scheme put forth in the Bill was , the antithesis of Sociali.m. llue Socialists did not like the idea of ownership became, they were doing all they otuld to bring about the ustionslisation of land in England. Their fellow-Socialists in Germany,however, I although they were making great headway, appeared to have very little in common with them in that , respect. Ownership struck a deeper root than I tenancy and a Nyinem of peasant proprietary would form a hasis for true democrecy. From a sociological point of view, it would bring out the moral and physical fibre of a man. It was far better for people to be employed on the land than in the mill. It would also make them more fit to undertake the defence of the Empire in the Army or the Navy. Coder the Bill advances would be made at • small rate of interest and facilities would be given not only to the large owners hut to the smaller cultleators. Another method of working such • scheme wee by the formation of co-operative banks but in that case it won Id also he neceosary to provide a State patentee, and he thoutht it would be better for the State to take the entire risk as it had done in Ireland. If a peasant pro- prietary could be created In Ireland, he saw no difficulty in the way of adopting a similar policy in this country. It was sdmitted by both parties that something should he done to bring the people back to the land. The difference between them wan that whereas the Coaservative plan FM to create small occupying ownen,the Liberal policy was one of small holdings. He was afraid there was little tope for the latter policy being successful. Experience proved that small holders had to pay very moo:, higher rents in order to cover any loss that might occur to the County Council. Moreover, it was impossible for a County or Borough Council to he an ideaL landlord. One of the I chief cause. of the decline oregriculture in England was the free importation of farm products. The agri- cultural interest appeared to have been absolutely neglected in favour of the manufacturing interest. No I other country imported the amount of foodatuffs that we did, and no other country was so dependent upon that source of supply. But there was undoubtedly .cope for a greater cultivation of the soil In England, and if It could be brought about much more money would to opent in the country. Mr. W. HARTLEY (C., Skipton), seconded the 'don- . . . . .

lion of the Bill. The scheme, he said, was inevitable end it would be for the public good. As the country became more thickly populated, land which formerly we. held up for sporting and ornamental purposes waft bound to come into the market. (Opposition cheers). That would result in the breaking op of large estates, which might lion bad thing for certain CiSSWII—in fact he did not consider it would be an unmixed blessing to lose the beautiful country seats of which England was so proud. In the early days the land of England was owned by the King—which might have been the best thing at a time when the country wee no sparsely populated. Eventually, it hethmo impossible for one man to control it, so he divided it amongst his barons and duke.. As the population increased, they appor- tioned the hod amongst the knights and sqnires, who in turn divided it amongst the fanners. Now the day of the large farmer was coming to an end and in tine the land would probably be out up into small holdings of from 3 to 30 acres. The land would then produce more and it would be better for the workers in the towns. (Opposition cheers). It was • matter of public net:lenity that the land should come into nthrket. (Opposition 0h0...) The policy of the Bill was quite rie inevitable as the democratising of the House of Lords (Opposi- tion chners)or the downfall of Free Trade. Government cheers and laughter. ,The Radical plan of dealing with the question was to create expeneive administration and • army of official,. The Conservative policy was make England free and to cut down officialdom. County Councils had taken over land, the tenant found that they were much better off coder' eyetem of landlords. PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

SMALL HOLDINGS.

The growth of small holdings inevitably means in turn, the growth of the poultry industry. lam not an advocate of renting laud purely for the purpose of breeding poultry. but rather that it should be an auxiliary industry ; for while some are able to make poultry farming pay many others fail in the attempt. A great many people get wrong impressions about the capabilities f few hens. Indeed they think that if they erect a few poultry houses and runs and stock them with fowls the rest is easy. Do not he misled 'dear readers, for such is not possible. It is Most difficult tend only achieved by a few people) to make a good living from poultry farming pure and simple. It is because of the many failure. (through wrong impressions) that we,as a productive nation, have not succeeded au we ought to have done. We are only just beginning to realise this fart, and one rightly asks for au alternative for pa,t failures. In France (as must be in England) it is the small-holder who makes and will make the most succeasful poultry farmer. It is

I lIE lIIIITIsII

of the future, equipped with all the modern and scientific methods, who will make poultry farming -pay. For such there is a great future. Ifere ! Amidst da under-supplied and teeming populdion there is 'much scope. If we cm only persuade the British Government to fairly (not partially) unlock the land we shall hear less of migration and unemployment. The most profitable place for a poultry farm is near an industrial centre. But look at the rent of the land ! It is impossible to rent such land for success- ful poultry farming. What are the consequences Well ! you have to get further afield where land is cheaper and here again you are handicapped by excessive railway rates in carrying your productn to market towns. It is because of these immediate difficulties that one urges the ernallholder to prmwed with the industry in coujunctiou with other industries that already pay.

- . PRESENT DAY SISMIS. A. little advice to the small-holder as to the best system to adopt will be useful. A few years hence I ook forward to each competition in the p ultry industry than at present i.revails. Therefore with this advantage it would pay boat to cater for home markets. What I mean is, that seeing the operations of a small-holding would be somewhal limited, there would be no need for an extensive s)stem of adver- tising. It is often the case that large farmers are ready purchasers of good stock when produced near home. Where stock and sittings of eggs could be thus disposed of, it would certainly effect a great saving to the small-holder.---....

'WEEDS To xr.Er.

....... First, then the small holder should estimate what breeds would be most suitable for his or her district. You Can generally tell what breeds are in greatest command by those already kept. If in your own mind they are not useful and kept in ignoranca, then break down the prejudice. and introduce into your own district more profitable stock. One salient feature which must ever guide you, is climatic condit ons and ! the nature of the soil. However moot! you may prefer one variety over another, if the land in not suitable it would be folly to continue to breed such. First get to understand what variety beat suits the soil, and then gb in for that or such like varieties. But you say they are not no good as much a breed. Surely if they are best adapted for the soil they would always do better than a better variety unnaturally suited and accommodated. Here is where scientific methods would have to be adopted. This strain you would , have to work en and perfect. Scientifically mated land bred, almost any popular breed of poultry could be made to pay.

In every industry B...afv;ity; pays VI specialise; providing always that you are sure of a demand for your speciality. If you find a demand for Wyandottes then go in for Wyandotms and no other. Slick to , the one breed and perfect it and in few years your none., with that of Wyandottea will be ineeperable ; thus you have made a name which by the way is half the battle. I always ask when looking at a pen of pure bred fowls, whose strain are they ? But I must discontinue now, I want the space at my command for other purposes. I will continue ou Small Holdings in next week's issue.

Is intemperance really on the increase in War- rington ? However that may be all available statistical and other information goes to chow that the consumption of spirits in the country as a whole has in the course of tbe last ten years or so fallen to shunt one-half what it was when the last census was taken, and that leer drinking is alai on the decline. Neverthe- less such titbits as the following are still in vogue : John Homphreys at a sapper drank more than was good for him, and arrived home at 2. am. in a rather unfortunate Mate. The family physician met him at the front door. " Ihimphreys, ' he BAK " I con- gratulate you. You're a father again," and led thimpheyn upstairs to the nursery, where the nurse proudly exhibited a fine infant. But Humphreys, instead i f inanifesting Itoooeveltian joy, frowned, swore, and left the room. The next in,ning, when be again saw the newborn child, lie showed amaze- ment mut perplexity. " But, 1111,80,” lie anti, " where is the other one'?" Sußt r just anothero his time from " Fry's Magazine." For many weary months the parson bad had great trouble with Jackson. It did not contain even each " temperance beer "as occasioned a licensing inquiry at our Police Court the other day ; it represented an avowedly stronger brew. " Wha is that for ? " asked the clergyman. "Father, sir," replied the girl. The vicar looked grave. " Lor bless your heart, sir," replied the maiden, "lather don't drink this beer. Ile soaks his bread in it!" EXECRABLE CALIGRAPHY.

That the handwriting of a great many men of this generation is inexcusably vile the proof readers and compositors on the stsff of this or any other journal will readily and with no little show of indignation and impatience testify. I was once in attendance at the , fortnightly meeting of a certain Board of Guardians! when the workhouse master's estimates were produced by the clerk. They included the medical officer's requisition respecting drugs for the dispensary, the list, which was in the doctor's own handwriting, being submitted precisely as it had come to band. The fact was that the master had been unable to make head or tail of it, and the clerk likewise found it quite undecipherable. Then the Chairman, posing as something of a handwrit- ing expert, studied it minutely with knitted brows, and finally pronounced it absolutely illegible. There- upon, the author himself was summoned and invited to read out the items. With a contemptuous sniff, he essayed no to do, but soon found himself in difficult ties and floundering pitiably, being ultimately com- pelled to the humiliating confession that he was 'unable to make out the characters he had himself set down. After all, affectation goes far to account for that Sort if thing. Downright ignorance, always deplorable, one at the time pities but the slipshod handiwork of the ready writer who is habitually carPlees causes more strong language on the part of unfortunate printers than he trots of or ever suspects. But with whim should the responsibility for these two f requent outbursts lie? In this connection I think of the excuse made on behalf of Uncle Toby ; under extreme provocation he committed himself upon which it was surmised that "The Accusing Spirit which flew to Heaven's chancery with the oath blushed as he gave it ie, and the Recording Angel an he wrote it dean dripped a tear upon the word that blotted it

out for ever !" I alluded to correspondents afflicted with what is technically called Scriveners palsy - thou3h for th it matter professional performeru on keyboard and oi rota, of the Mt iaged instruments are liable to and out n !suffer from this distressing complaint. As those who do me the honour rrgulally to cat an eye over this column sill have noticed, I like to take into my absolute con- fidence and to chat intimately with my readers,even to the extent of occasionally t•oubling them with purely personal matter. Now I happen t i have been sorely handicapped by writer's cramp for well nigh 30 years, and lam glad to think that, with the charity that suffereth long and is kind, my comrades on the " Examiner " staff make considerable allowances for m on this account. IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

HOME RULE OPTIMISM.

Competent and independent observers of' the Irish race have generally regarded them as a people whose natural temperament is that of the optimist. And perhaps that is why in the yearly celebrations of thcir National Festival of St. Patrick's Day, whcsi the scattered children of the Gael meet together, no matter what the national nut- look may be, there is ever the rising hope for the homeland. In the dark and almost hopeless days of the Parnell split and in the promising position of last year it was the same, cheerful enthusiastic atmosphere that marked the gatherings of the Gaels on their patron Saint's Day. But in no hour of the past, no matter how rosy its promise, was there such justification for high hopes for the Irish as the present. Are we over-sanguine when we write here that the cause of Ireland and the Irish has never occupied a position of greater power and strength than that in which it stands to-day? Far from being over-sanguine it is the barest statement of fact. We see in the first view a Home Rule Premier, guiding a Home Rule Cabinet in destroying the citadel of Lordly aggression I and in curbing the powers of the privileged few,breaking fetters that bind the many. We learn, on Cabinet authority,that his Majesty's ministers are engaged not in mere pious expressions of Home Rule faith but in serious and systematic investigations into the fundamental features of the new Home Rule scheme. In the camp of the enemy, con- fusion alternating with charlatan experi- ment at misrepresenting the Irish, is the order of the day. But what is perhaps greater than all in the contemporary position is the fact that all over Great Britain, the justice of the Home Rule cause has caught tho conviction of the democratic mind in a way that not even Gladstone and Parnell knew. Liberal and Labour forces h ive joined together on this paramount question on the domestic politics of the Kinglmi and to balance the bluster of the Coercioeists we find on all sides clear thinking and active doing for a satisfactory and defi ni te settlement of an age-hong problem. Acoss the water, in Ireland itself, the same cheerful prospect opens out. Local Government pre- paring the people for the larger sphere of national activities ; Education, whilst far from ideal, still being brought nearer to the people's needs and aspiration; and temperance, intellectual and social movements giving greater force to the civic sense and the national character. Truly it is good for us Irishmen to be living in a day so big with hope for a cause consecrated by strivings and sacrifices I stretching over a chain of centuries. But whilst our hopes are greater to-day so in I exact proportion is increased the responsi- bilities of every Irishman who believes his dream of an Irish Parliament is shortly to be realised. There is work for every Irishman, especially the Irishman in England, between the present and the long prayed-for consum- mation of Irish antonomy. And we can be of good cheer in the thought that it is ours to be in at this work and that in another celebration or two yet of St. Patrick 's Day, when we toast the old toast of " Ireland a Nation," we can stand up to that honoured call as men who lent a good hand in the transition from wish to achievement. THE LATEST TORY TARADIDDLE.

The latest campaign of Unionist vilification opened a few days ago in the House of Commons in a muddled attempt on the part of a few carrion crows, to borrow Mr. Birrell's description of the Ulster Unionist politicians, to show that the administration of the Irish Law Courts is now carried on to favour Catholics and to injure Protestants. The affair, it was clear, had been carefully worked up, and as usual was a complete failure. A long and exaggerated speech by Mr. William Moore drew from the Irish Attorney General a spirited and courageous rejoinder which clearly proved the whole charge to be stuffed with a series of misrep- resentations and misstatements of fact. It was early in the day. The House had been sitting all night, and most of its members were weary. Mr. John Redmond plunged into the fray with a vigour and intensity of feeling that quickly roused to wild enthusiasm the jaded members of the Irish and Ministerial benches. The public would see, he said, the real object of the speeches of Mr. Moore and Lord Castlereagh. A scheme was being launched by those who were opposed to the concession of liberty to Ireland, with the object of blackening the name of Ireland, and defaming the action of her people. Turning to Lord Castlereagh, though the Irish leader is rarely heard to sound a personal note, Mr. Redmond, in a few crushing sentences, reminded the noble Lord that he would do well to be particularly careful about associating himself with slandering campaigns of this kind, for which Lord Castlereagh appeared, however, to have a special taste. His name was indissolubly linked with the vilest crimes in Ireland that had ever been perpetrated against a nation. The passion and fervour with which Mr. Redmond spoke excited both sides of the House in no ordinary way and at the close of his telling speech, he had a perfect ovation. The Tories next turned their personal rancour on Mr. Churchill, who, of course, hit them back to their doubled dismay. At one point Captain Craig angrily asked the permission of the chair to turn out the, whole of the Nationalists who were backing the Govern- ment in the fight. The exhibition of valour on the part of the retired militia captain caused only less amusement in a tired House than Mr. Jeremiah MacVeagh's witty description of Lord Castlereagh, Lord Helmsley and Lord Hugh Cecil as " the noble triplets "—a designation that seems likely to stick to this strange combination of aristocrats.

TALKS ON HEALTH.

ph A FAMILY DOCTOR.]

TO THOSE ABOUT TO MARRY. Well, Edwin and Angelina, I hear you are going to get married this year, and I offer you my good wishes. I sympathise with yot, in the unfortunate circumstance that every- one you meet givria you good advice; good advice is terribly boring to young people. However, it happens to be my Imeinen to help the British public to keep the doctor out of the house. Are you quite sure that your health is WI good as you ran make it% I know how hard you have to work, and that you cannot always choose the way of life you aro compelled to lead, but are you doing your beat? Edwin, if you stand before the altar and solemnly take oath that you will cherish Angelina, you are belying your oath the moment von take it if you bring to your marriage a -feeble stock of health. lam very leased to know you have saved up a tme Pof money; now take • little more trouble to save up a store of health.

-: 0 : GOOD WISHES.

Dear Angelina, the years go by very quickly; in ten years there will be others to look after besides Edwin ; pray that those others may be strong and healthy. Ask Edwin ta join a Friendly Society, no that something will lie corning in if he is ill. Re- member, Angelina, that nothing ruins a home like an unhealthy husband; and an for you. Edwin, there will be 00 excuse if a young man like von falls ill. CRUSADE AGAINST CONSUMPTION. At the present time a fierce crueade ii being waged against consumption, and I want to be in it. The crusade in under the auspices of the National Society for the Prevention of Consumption, and the main object is to enlighten and educate the people on the methods of prevention. Ignorance is to be dispersed and indifference banished. Lecturea will be given. and an exhibition opened free to the public. The microbes of consumption are beginning to shiver in their shoes. THE FIRST PROCESS.

In the manufacture of any article it is the first process that is of greatest importance: if the first prooese is badly done all subse- quent stages of manufacture must be corre- spondingly defective. Our bodies are manu- factories, and we have to make brain, bone, and muscle (especially brain, please) out of -ggs and bacon and bread end butter. How can you expect this wonderful process to be properly contrived if you have bad teeth! The first prooess is mastication, and if you have no grinders you throw a lot of extra work on the stomach and liver; and when these organe complain you start your favourite pastime of thug-swilling. To put a bottle of medicine in your stomach be- eaune you have no grinders is folly. Have your mouth put in order, and your indiges- tion will disappear, provided you have not delayed too long. As for the man who has foul teeth, with a septic discharge around them, I give him up in deepair.

o : ROOM FOR IMPROVEMENT.

I was reading a book of reminiscences by an old lady of eighty-four recently, and noticed her remark that bat h-rooms were unknown in country houses in her child- hood. How shocked we all are to read that! We have made some headway since eighty yearn ago. Nearly every house haft a bath flow: some people find it very convenient to put the coalw in. In another eighty years what • will people be saying of us and our habits of cleanlinesm% I think there is no doubt that the condition of the heads of school-children, as net out in the official records, will be regarded with amazement and abhorrence, to the everlasting shame of modern parents. Hundreds of thousands of English mothers would rather have their children dirty than clean, and hundreds of nurses have to be employed for no other purpose than to examine the children. Cats take much endless trouble to keep their kittens dean. Darwin said We were de-

mended from animals: if only We could amend to their level again !

A LESSON IN BREATHING.

I said a week or two ago that many. of you do not know how to breathe, and a correspondent asks me to explain further. The reason I brought this accusation against you was that when a pupil begins a course of singing lessons the breathing exercises generally, give rise to a headache or giddiness. 1% hen the pupil has learnt how to take full breaths the blood in aerated in a more complete and satisfactory manner, and, far from having a headache, the head feels clearer. The breath should be taken through the nose, IRCIIIIAC the air is warmed and filtered of floating impurities: the chest most expand all round by giving free play to the ribs (ladies with tight corsets please mute), and the diaphragm. that sheet of muscle which separates the chest cavity from the abdomen, must descend. This diaphragm, or mid-riff, gives us the link between immiration and digestion. If the stomach in blown out with wind or occupied by a surfeit of turkey and plum-pudding, the diaphragm in unable to descend, and the coneerynence is that the diaphragm is im- peded, and the breath is not taken properly.

PUTTING YOUR FOOT DOWN.

The foot in an arch, in fact, we rest our weight on only three !mintn in the sole of the foot—the ball of the big toe, the ball of the little toe, and the heel. Thuo the foot is constructed to give springiness to the gait and to reduce the shock in jumping, running, and walking. In coming down on your foot, even in ordinary walking, with your eleven stone of weight, the jerk would be considerable if it were not for the care- fully-planned mechanism of the bones and ligaments.

PENALTY 01 ILL-FITTING BOOTS

My complaint is that you upset the mechanism of the foot by wearing ill-fitting boote. "Oh," you say, "wonderful meeha- nisru, is it? We will noon alter that." And you proceed to screw your feet up until they are deformed, or, what in far wor,,e, you take the delicate, soft-boned feet of your little children and distort them. Result Bunions, corns, fiat-foot, in-growing toe- nail, hammer-toe, rheumatism. For grown- up people who deform their even feet the punishment is three months' without the option, but for those who ruin the feet of helpless children it is sin months' hard labour and a shameful reputation amongst the more thoughtful parents in the neigh- bourhood.

This was the third meeting of the season between the reams, and once again the Reds failed to defeat Wan ington. Waiting for the bounce merely gives the other side time to spread out, and occasionally it spells danger.

AN INTERESTING SUGGESTION,

A correspondent sends me the following interesting suggestion : Now that the football season is drawing to a close might I suggest that a ninth be arranged with the veterans of the Warrington club, the entire proceeds to be devoted to the Infirmai y Million Penny Fund. I am ...ure that a match on such lines would be the mean, of providing a substantial RUM towards the Infiimarv. Failing thii. could not a match 1* arranged between /0,4 players of the present time and taported players. Barnes, Isherwood, Moores; Ashurat and Houghton ; Seholtze, Eden, Mason, Swill, I'. Hill and J. Fairhurst. b„it. li,, cc- —Mclntyre: ; Waywell, Dickenson, .1. One. if not both of these matches, should be practic- able, and personally I incline to the Veterans match as a source of attraction. Anyhow I hope the Club Com- mittee will give my correspondent's suggestion the consideration it deserves, if only to help on the deserving cause of charity for which the Club has always done so much so willingly.

School Fen and Compulsory Night Schools Condemned.

Mr. George Welker, representative of the WorLing- men's Infirmary Conunittee on the Education Com- mittee f .resented his oth annual report at the annual meeting held at the Town flail cm Saturday afternoon Dealing more particularly with education in War- rington, the report stated that locally we were still , faced with the fact that our school accommodstion was not equal to the number of children requiring to ,be taught. Schools were overcrowded. We were fre- quently receiving letters from the Board of Education threatening the witholding of money grants unless that elate of things was remedied. Our two Council schools were overcrowded, and voluntary schools, which had effectively taught hundred, of children in the past, tinder wortes conditions than now obtained in them, had had their certified numbers greatly reduced by the exacting requirements of the than of I.:location. THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY. MARCH 18 1911.

The speech made by

SIR E. GREY'S Sir Edward Grey on GREAT SPEECH. Monday night has

commanded the close and serious attention of all the Press of the civilized world. We are least satisfied, we must admit, with the views expressed in Paris. The Foreign Secretary was merely using words, words, words, says one Parisian journal. France and England, says another. have other things to do than to exchange vows of eternal friendship. What a certain part of French journalism wants— which is not necessarily wanted by French Statesmanship—is not an arrange- ment which would secure Peace, but an alliance on terms which might drag us into war which did not involve our actual interests. We are pleased to observe, how- ever, that at least one Parisian newspaper devoted to the cause of the common people highly approves of what was said by Sir Edward Grey, and regards his words as likely to produce a good and salutary effect. What is said by one of the Vienna journals focuses the amazement which must be felt in Europe pretty generally at the continual building of more and larger ships. As a member of the Triple Alliance, Austria is beginning to take a part in the great Dread- nought competition. Austria-Hungary, says the Vienna "Free Press," has been drawn against her will into the whirlwind of com- petitive naval armaments, and feels the weight of the financial burden—feels it at the very beginning. The people of the Dual King- dom wonder, no doubt, meanwhile, why they should be building huge ships of war. Any political expectations that they may have lie southwards, and cannot be helped towards realisation by means of ships. The Dual Monarchy, says our Austrian contemporary, will certainly be willing to support the efforts of the English Foreign Secretary to find means of slackening the pace of arma- ments. All Europe, we do not hesitate to

say, would be of the same opinion outside official 'circles. Why does the mad com- petition continue? The answer to that question may be found in a Berlin journal which says that Great Britain's Naval Budget is directed primarily against Germany. That is precisely interchanging the name of the two counties. It is what is , being said on this side of the North Sea by many heated politicans about the German programme of shipbuilding. And it is Germany and Great Britain which set the pace. One great effect of SIR EDWARD GREY'S speech will, we believe, be to stimulate self- examination in this country and in others as to why we should talk so much about peace and should simultaneously act so much as if we were living in apprehension of the imme- diate outbreak of war. This is an era of Hague Conferences, of noble Peace prizes, of societies whose object it is to foster amity among nations. But it is still more conspic- ously the age of rivalry in arms, and of an almost generally prevailing impression that the circumstances of the time demand a larger and wider expenditure in armaments. Our Colonies or some of them at least, are raising armies on their own, building vessels of war, and assuring the Mother Country that they will be at hand when they are needed. The Mother country nods grateful recognition, and proceeds to increase her estimates for Army and Navy, just as if the Colonies were doing nothing 'whatever. That is the case with ourselves, and we may see our own fever and fret reflected elsewhere. The American Continent bristles with Navies which are never likely to be needed. We are I actually spurred on to the building of bigger warships by the orders which have been given to British shipyards by some South American Republic. _

An English poet said of NAPOLEON soon I after his death,

"And eomewbere now in yonder mare, He sees what earthly glory in."

If we could thus see ourselves' from the out- side, and at a distance, how improbable it would seem that human beings could behave as the more civilised nations of the world are now doing I The cause of it all lies in the almost universal feeling that there must be war because there is so much preparation for it. It is as if we were putting ourselves into the hands of a blind fate. A prophet greatly daring might more than probably earn a great future reputation, by declaring that almost all the arms that are now being made will rot away without use. If they do not, there is far more likelihood of the revolutioe spoken of by Sir EDWARD GREY—th uprising against armaments—than of any European Armageddon.

The House of Lords THE DECLARATION debate on the Declara- EA:OF LONDON. tion of London was carried over from last week till Monday. The more the Declara- tion is understood, the better for the Govern- ment. It relates, of course, both to our belligerent rights and to the protection of food supplies in neutral vessels. The Admiralty argues that it will not restrict to , any appreciable extent our belligerent rights. Instead of exposing our food supplies to risk in time of war, it gives a security which we never had before.

The Member for War-

MR. HAROLD rington seems deter SMITH, M.P., AND mined to be "in the THE TRADE, picture," and if he goes on catching the Speaker's eye at his present rate we shall be hearing that Mr. Lowther and Mr. Emmott have taken to wearing coloured spectacles in order to tone down the Smithsonian brilliance. Mr. Harold is treading rapidly in the steps of" F.E." and during the few short weeks of the present session he has made enough appearances on the Parliamentary stage to suggest that the family reputation for modesty is not going to suffer at his hands. There is none of the "new hand" about the Member for Warrington, and the competence which he is already displaying for single- handed administration of the affairs of the nation would be surprising in a member of any other family. We note that he has given notice to move quite a batch of amendments to the Revenue Bill in Committee. They indicate Mr. Smith's tender affection for the Trade and gratitude for its bounteous help in times of trouble. Briefly, they concern the proportionate pay- ments of duty by licensees on commencing or terminating a tenancy in the middle of a quarter,and aim at giving the licensee special consideration which is not granted to people in other walks of life. The amend- ments strike us as being trivial and unnecessary, and very unlikely to commend themselves to this or any other Government. There is no hardship on the publican if he goes about with his eyes open. Mr. Smith has also found a tiny flaw in the Parliament I Bill, and will seek an opportunity to rise and ' propose that the important words "for public purposes" should be inserted after the word " loan " in the second paragraph of the first clause. The meaning is quite clear and obvious without the amendment, but it does not satisfy our fastidious member. If he has no other objection to the Parliament Bill, however, we will readily congratulate him on a sound appreciation of the national mandate expressed therein.

The past year's work WORKINGMEN AND of the Workingmen's THE INFIRMARY. Committee of the

Warrington Infirmary affords a striking illustration of the value of systematic collections in the local works. The income from outside sources, not under the direct control of the committee, showed a falling off as compared with last year, but this was more than counter-balanced by the increase of .£lO7 2s. 2d. in the amount received from the employees at the various places of business. Congratulations are due to the committee upon their successful exertions which have resulted in the sum of £1231 3s. ld.being handed over to the treasurer of the , Infirmary as the workingmen's contribution I for 1910. There is, however, still room for improvement. Had it not been for the systematic methods of subscribing which ' have been adopted in several establish- ments the total receipts would have been less than in the previous year. The moral is obvious. The employees of all the works, large and small, could, without any undue strain upon their resources, increase the regular income of the institution to such an extent that it would not be apt to suffer seriously by a decline in the revenue from extra efforts. A contribution of id. per

week per head from all who are in full work is a modest sum, but the pennies quickly mount up and this scheme would count very appreciably in the bulk. The annual sub- scription list of the Warrington Infirmary compares well with that of any similar institution in England, but the workingmen's contributions do not stand in the came favourable light. With a more determined and organized movement, however, the reproach may soon be removed, and we believe that the coming year will witness a great accession of interest in this important committee's activities.

No one who has per- SMALL-PDX. sonal recollections of the small-pox epidemic in Warrington in 1892 can view with unconcern the re-appearance of the dread malady in various parts of the country, and particularly in Lancashire. There is already something like an epidemic out- burst ,in Bury, where 23 cases have developed in a very few days ; and Wigan has also got two or three sufferers within its borders. This is coming perilously near, and must necessarily involve increased watchfulness and some anxiety on the part of those who are responsible for the public health locally. One cannot help recalling the utterance of the Mayor at the February Council meeting, as being somewhat remark- able in view of subsequent develop- ments. His Worship said in the course of a discussion regarding the isolation of phthisis patents, that "War- rington was so well vaccinated that we had not a good chance of having, if we wanted one, a good smallpox epidemic." The words and the tone were both reassuring, but we doubt if they would be uttered now, I with the enemy practically knocking at the I gates. We have no wish to be alarmist, but lit has perhaps been forgotten that the I number of exemptions from vaccination has been increasing every year, and there must be many people in Warrington who would be susceptible to contagion. A study of the history of small-pox epidemics before and since Edward Jenner's wonderful discovery leaves no room for doubting the wisdom, nay, imperative necessity of vaccination, and the present outbreak of the disease in various parts of the country should act in the nature of a warning to the sceptical. We sincerely hope that Dr. Joseph's optimism may be justified by the immunity of War- rington people. A repetition of 1892, even on a small scale, is not to be contemplated without much perturbation.

One of the most interesting of Warrington's ancient buildinga—the Olde Foxe Inn—will shortly disappear from Buttermarket-street to allow of the widening of that extremely narrow and congested thoroughfare. We I hear that a suggestion has been made to the Street Improvement Committee that the constituents of the structure should be pre- served and the inn re-erected in the form of a shelter in Victoria Park. The idea is a novel one and it would certainly be prefer- able to the entire demolition of this pictur- esque relic of old times. _

In a recent number of the " American Magazine" an article appeared from the pen of Miss Ida M. Tarbell, entitled "The Mysteries and Cruelties of the Tariff," in the 1 course of which she gives an account of a I conversation which took place not long ago on the deck of a White Star liner. A New York stockbroker asked a fellow passenger, who hailed from the West Riding, and had been connected with the woollen and worsted industries all his life, to examine a suit of clothes he was wearing, and tell him what "a gentleman "would have to give for such a suit in England. On examination it was found that the cloth did not contain a particle of wool, but was altogether com- posed of cotton and shoddy. The York- shireman informed his fellow-passenger that no gentleman in England would wear such a suit, but that a better one could be bought at Leeds for a guinea and a half, and a very much better one for two guineas. The American had given his New York tailor 371 dollars, or, say, CI 10s. for this shoddy suit.

In these days, when the dignity of the Speaker is so jealously guarded, one reads' with horror of the strange liberties taken 1 with one of his august predecessors of a past century, as recorded in the old Journals of the House of Commons. Thus, in July, 1610, we read it was "affirmed by the Speaker that Sir Edward Herbert put not off his hat to him, but put out his tongue, and popped his mouth with his finger in scorn." Sir Edward stood up and said that he meant no offence to the House; and when he added that "he had not intent of scorn," Mr. Speaker professed himself "worse satisfied than before." Two days later the House was informed by Mr. Speaker that "Mr. T. T—, in a. loud and violent manner, and contrary to the usage of Parliament, stand- ing near the Speaker's Chair, cried 'Ba w ' in the Speaker's ear, to the great terror and affrightment of the Speaker and of the members of the House." In this case the offender had the decency to tender a full apology. -

In another column we publish a transla- tion of an article from ths pen of Professor Dr. von Engel, of Berlin, in ' Die-Woche," I • which shows how clearly the arguments in favour of the Daylight Saving Bill are • appreciated abroad, and how reasonable it is to expect that when

a Daylight Saving Act is in force in England, a similar movement of the clocks will take place on the Continent. When that happens of course the various nations will all stand in relation to one another with regard to standard time, as they do to-day. • . • Intelligent consideration would seem to have been given (says" Country Life") to the questions which we shall all have to answer on our Census papers at the end of the week after next. With every new decade the necessity for trustworthy statistics becomes greater, and there is a large variety of men waiting to use the figures. The additional particulars that will be requested on this occasion in regard to the profession or occupation of persons aged ten years and upwards will give information invaluable to politicians and social reformers of all shades of opinion. Another point on which the directors of the Census manifest a great thirst for knowledge is the size and com- position of families. _

We are glad to not: that the parishioners of Stockton Heath have found wisdom, and that at a meeting on Tuesday evening they negatived a proposal to discuss the question of seeking urban powers, by an overwhelm- ing majority. The Chairman's comment that ' the time is not yet" was apt and to the point. It is hardly an open question whether the time ever will come. In all probability Stockton Heath will eventually be absorbed by the County Borough of Warrington, a fate which some of the leading lights of the big village view with a horror which accords ill with the fact that they nearly all earn their bread and butter in and obtain their greatest everyday conveniences from the borough. A New York Correspondent gives a very I instructive extract from a speech made in the American Senate. Senator M'Cumer, as an example of the high cost of living in the United States, incidentally gave an expense account of a trip to New York, using the products of farms instead of dollars as follows :

ONE DAY IN NEW YORK. Cab to hotel, six bushel, of oats. Tip to driver, fifteen cabbages. Tip to elevator boys, two dozen eggs. Tip to bell-boy, 11 bushels of barley. Breakfast, quarter ton of hay. Tip to waiter, two bushels of potatoes. Luncheon, one sheep. Tip to waiter, l bushels of carrots. Dinner, four bushels of rye. Tip to waiter, one bushel of onions. Room, ball a car of turnips. By the death of Mr. John Berry, manager of the Central Hotel, florsemarket-atreet, Warrington, a well-known personality in the local sporting world, has been removed. The Second visit 716; seas mof Mr 7. F. Mahler, of I Salons on Tuesday last the occasion of elector. on "The Neckar, Moselle, and Rhine" pictured in 180 lantern elides. He names the G.C.R. route via Hull and Rotterdam as the most convenient from Warring- ton ; thence across Holland to Dusseldorf where the boat is taken up the Rhiae. The river traffic is immense and the boats, chiefly for cargo, have good , passenger accommodation. From Heidelberg he took on by rail to Coblenz and then by light railway to Metz, as it takes two days to go up the river, though only one to come down. He described Metz very fully, especially the national memorial erected by Germany to commemorate the war of 1870-71. We came by boat down the Moselle picking up cases of wine, cbiefly as our cargo, and admiring the eirnations of the many ruined castles, ruined since 1870 when they were destroyed by the French. The large Rhine steamer from Coblenz down to Dusseldorf is eplen- didlv fitted up—a miniature ocean liner. Mr. Mahler was heartily Thanked at the close by a full gathering. The announcement will be read with regret by I many Warrington people of the death of Mrs. McCreadie. who for many years was the licensee of the White Hart Hotel, Warrington, and was respected by a large circle ef friends in this town. Mrs. MoCreadie took charms of the boom on the death of her husband, Mr. William McCreadie. who previously held the licence. She left Warrington nine years ago and took up her .abode at the Haven Hotel, Farodca, near Chaster, which was the venue of many parties of pleasure seekers from this distrrm during the summer months. Mrs. McCreadie had been in failing health for sometime, mffering limn a serious internal com- plaint, but really no alarm was experienced until a fortnight ago, whet an operation was decided upon. She rallied, but there was an unfortunate relapse and death ensued. She leaves one eon and two daughters to mourn her loss. The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday.

Tone. AN UNPOPULAR NOTICE. Notice boards similar to the one in the photograph have been erected on all approach roads to the Knutsford-road Swing Bridge, Latchford. Residents in the district will be put to considerable inconvenience during the time the Bridge is closed, from Monday next to Sunday, the 26th inclusive. To cross the Canal they will have to make a detour of nearly a mile via the Cantilever Bridge. The cause of all the trouble is the cracking of one of the cylinders on which the Bridge swinge, and it will take a week to replace it with a new one.

DAYLIGHT SAYING.

HOW THE B!LL IS REGARDED IN GERMANY.

What is a Philiatine ? Of at least a hundred definitions the one which pleases me the beet is the Philistine says to every new and sensible pro- posal, idiotic nonsense. He amuses himself over everything and anything that be has not been cream. towed to see, hear, etc., oppises any alteration in his :common-ghee way of life; finds everything absunl that anyone else does because 7 is different from what Ihe does himself ; is the mortal enemy of all progress. I but enjoys it when it has been carried through." In FallSt it 18 said " We are tined to people despising what they do not understand " and I am sure the idea of an increase in the enjoyment of the sunlight . by mankind, which is none being seriously considered in England, will at first be received with laughter by the Philistine. of all natio,s. But this does not prevent the route Philistines from enjoying the advantages of the idea, forgetting the author of it, and behaving with the name indestructible philistiniSlD at the next opportunity. Sow for the facts of this case. A Member of the English House of C,onanons, the very praiseworthy T. W.1)06100, has brought in a Bill which proposes that on the third Sunday in April

ALL ILOeKA 1:11EAT DRITAIN SHALL DE ITT I'oRUARD I

and back again one hour on the third Sunday in Septetnber. 'Tho gain in sunlight and bright hours for work amounts to 154 hours in a year. Mr. Dobson said in opposition to the Philistinea who are naturaliy not lacking even in Parliaments, that be himself " at first considered the proposal to be laugh- able, absurd and impracticahle." There is more joy in heaves over one Philistine who repents, than over one man of progress who has not added to his knowledge. Neither in public nor in private life are there any insuperable difficulties to be overcome. The English railway companies estimate their saving in lighting expenses at 2 million marks, and their savings in other ways would be still greater. lam sure that after cosmopolitan philistinism has laughed at it a little, the law for enriching the life of man- kind by an hour's sunlight will penetrate everywhere, eventually also into Germany. T. bring this coda little nearer is the Object of these remarks. At present who rules the time of our getting tip and going to bed? Simply the clock, and the cnatonis we regulate by it. The position of the sun has as good as nothing to do with the beginning and end of our day of work and recreation. Sunrise and sunset have no connec- tion with the so-called beginning and end of our day of work and recreation. The limits of our day are decided by ens urn, and not by the position of the sun. There is. there- , fore, not the klightest natural inner necessity for them. If in the summer the clock be put forward an hour, and all arrangements of the day are performed in , obedience with the hour which has been put nn. we ' shall gain daily a while hour's sunlight. Now it is clear that, unless they are forced into it, no entire population of any country will ever decide to rise an hour earlier than usual, arid correspondingly to go to bed earlier. All friendly advice, all grounds of common sense, all the clear advantages of early rising, convince no one who is not couvineed without them. Mankind is governed far less by common sense, than by a number of insignificant, imponderable, very often entirely senseless impulses and customs. . . If with the agreement of the German Kingdoms, and the approval of the Reichstag, the clocks were put on an hour, from April let to September 311th, no one would talk of it any mote. Even the Philistines would not understand bow the Government could have beea stupid enough not to hit on this arrangement years ago. It needs no decisivv alterations in public life, the hours of service for the Collets would remain the same, inland railway time tables will not be altered, hours of work in manufactories, mercantile houses and banks, meal times in families, they would all remain as they are. Only

TIIE DirrEREN,E

would be that work in summer would begin an hour earlier by the sun, and end at an earlier hour by the sun, no that all men who work with their minds or their bands, will enjoy an hour more sunlight daily alter their work is done. Do not bold up against me that one of the results would be the shortening of our night's rest by an hour. Whoever gets up at seven instead of eight will of their own accord find the necessity of going to sleep an hour earlier. Our whole conceptions of daytime depend on our clocks. Do you think that after the introduction of Summer Season Time everyone will ray in the even- tug when the clock is at 111 or 11 really it is not no late, it is only ii or 10 " ? That would lie sheer nonsen,e, for there is no such " really." It is 10 or 11 because our clock and all other clocks say so Alao you may depend on it that the 4rtv lovers of nicht who made use of thin" really " would soon be forced to learn, be the overwhelming jr,wer of everyone else. that 12 is 12 and nothing else. Our whole private and public life in summer, after the introduction of this reform, will not only g.:in an hour's sunlight, but something invaluable in freshness. For all adult workpeople the advance of the hour means an hour's greater freshness in the morning and an extra hour's light in the afternoon. Workers of all kinds and officials would receive a whole hour more daylight for recreation and the occupations of family life. All this is no incontrovertible, an simple, that one must be a confirmed sceptic in order to discover insuperable difficulties.

No :;OVERN 4 ENT N 1.. AVEC EN. T, !TI

to make a whole nation rise at seven instead of eight in summer. No society, however power of, would be able to induces universal movement for early eking for increasing our enjoyment of the sunlight. But pint one short movement of the bands of our clock suffices. in this world of appearances to drive on earlier to bed aml earlier out of it.and it will be all the more certain the more universally thin movement % made. What annoys or saddens me • little about whole affair, apart from the envious feeling that did not think of the proposal myself. is the re/ that it did not at least start in Germany. The Er supporters of this gain in sunshine, have c shown their nati 'nal characteristics. An how daylight in aummer, means an hour longer Er in the open air. REV. H. V. PICOT, Rector of Orappenhall.

The ancient church of the quaint village of ( re ppen- hall Fe One of the most picturesquely eituated and historically interesting edifices in the Warrington district. Standing within the shadow of the old square tower, and enjoying the restful calm which only reigns in hamlets away from, the busy highroads, the visitor finds it hard to realize that such pleasant rural surroundings can exist unspoiled on the very outskirts of a manufacturing town like Warrington. The history of the pariah church has no often been written and lectured about that a very brief review will suffice here. Therein no mention of a church at Grappenhall in the Domesday sorvey of 1086, but it seems quite clear that a church in the Norman style wen erected early in the nextcentury. The date, as closely as it can be ascertained, was 1120. Of this buifilieg only a puritan of the smith wall remains, utel this, nub the corbel 4 ride and sculptured heads, such as were common at the period, is one of the most interesting features of the building. Another relic of the Norman church is the font, discovered under the floor of the nave in 1873. The tin-et eccleeiestic whose name le sesociated with the parish is one" Robert, parson of Grappenhall," who wrote and witnesaed a charter in 1189. About 1331 the Boydel Chapel was built. nail some .4 the glares remains to this day. In 1525 the tanner was erected and the church re-modelled in its present form. A few years later the south aisle win added. The fabric underwent careful restoration in 1873, under the care of the Rev. Thoinurs tin mall, for many years Rector of the parish. The present Rector is tine Rey. Harry Vernon Piga, M.A., who by Ida devoted labours successively in Watrington, Stoekton Heath end Grappenhall, hoe gained the affectionate regard not only of Churchmen but of representatives of all eectioneof religious order- prise. Mr. ingot was born on October 10th, lASI, his mother being the elder daughter of the late Mr. Peter Greenall, at nue time M.P. for Wigan. Having received his early education at the Liverpool College he proceeded to Oxford New College in 1873 and Oriel College in 1071. Ile took his RA. degree in 1077 and two years later entered tips the assistant mastership of the Appleby Grammar School, Westmorland, where he remained until 18811,takins his M.A. degree in 1885. Lie was ordained deacon In 1887 by the Bishop of Manchester and priest in 1888. Ills first curtcy was at Bolton-le-Sands, near Lancast-r, from 1887 to 1090, after which came a change from reral to induett iel life, Mr. Pigot being appointed curate of Christ Church Crewe, in 1890 and remaining there one year. It was in 1891 that he canny to Warrington as Vicer , of St. Paul's, und the excellent work which he did in that populous parish is fresh in the mind* of our readers. The church noon renovated internally and externally, seam of £7OO was seem on the erection of a large classroom for the girls' school in Rolleston- street, and the other schools were considerably improved. Mr. Pigot was appointed Vicar of St. Thomas', Stockton Heath in 1899 and under his care the usefulness and promerity of the church was fully maintained. The buiiding itself was enriched by the addition of a window in the eouth end to the memory of the late Mr. Robert Davie,, who had been actively identified with the church for a coneiderable number of yearn. The cost was borne by the parishioners and friends In 1905 Mr. Place became Rector of Grappenhall, Ids successor at Stockton Heath being the Rev. James Collins, late curate of Arniley, Leeds. From the outset Mr. Pigot gained the confidence and warm ' esteem of his new parishioners and he carries ont the manifold duties of a p trish clergyman most faithfully and well.

The Church and Politics.

A Chat With the Rev. A. J. Waldron.

The Bev. A. J. Waldron, vicar of Britton, who will preach at the Warrington Church Society's special services for women and men at the Parish Church on Friday next, is widely known as a vigorous and convincing champion of Christianity against the militant critics of the Foote end Itobcrtson type who have so much of their own way in the London Parks. Ile has also had many years etrerience of , the conditions of life amongst the London working chose., and is familiar with both the sctial and what : might be called the intellectual causes which affect • the question. In this week's "Great Thoughts," lfr. Will lam II Seed gives an interesting account of an interview with ' the rev. gentleman.

O th;eubject of polities in the pulpit, Mr. Waldron said :

--""Party politics ought to be barred from all pulpits. both Nonconformist and Church. Nonconformists have erred during the last few years, but we have erred for centuries. Look how most of the country puts inn have alwavn preached rank Toryism !" "But is it really possible to keep out politics? the interviewer argued. " Many a man thinks he in fulfilling a religious duty in advocating reforms of various sorts, such an poor law reform, the extension of old age pensions. iuternational peace, and what not. During the lest General Election I beard a Church parson in Paddington declaring that it was his duty , an a clergyman to preach 'Tariff Reform.' Many men , nowadays make a religion of their polities, and do not think they are doing wrong in doing :to. Is religion say use if it cannot be applied nationally." "Politica of • party nature ought to be kept out." he persisted. " The politic, of a national issue ought to be introduced. There are such questions as the opium traffic which ought to unite everybody. limn there is the necessity of preaching the Gospel of Peace. International arbitration ought to unite all men who go/to church. If it does not, the Church is not doing I[lo to much good. Of course, there are always people rfrho have no sense of proportion." rir "I am a known Radical. to the disgust of many of Imy people. Not a single leading layman of my church lis a Liberal, and they all say that I have never I alienated anybody by preaching Party politics. When , I refer to anything political in church I do so from the standard of national righteousness, and they never complain. But I always studiously avoid anything ,in the nature of a party issue. They have laughed and said I was too much of an idealist. but they have I never thought I preached it because I sass Radical. Some have left because I took a prominent place on Radical platforms outside."

The Church and Social Questions. The Bishop of Hull, speaking in Liverpool on Tuesday evening et a public meeting arranged by the i Christian Social Union, mid that social reform svas sulrering from the madness of political partivanship. The only measures likely to be teltee up were th^ee which hit a political opponent bard, and had to be forced one reluctant minority. The Bishop of Liverpool, mid the object of the Christian Social Union was not to nationalise property but to exorcise seltbihness, not to destroy the rights of property but to remind those who had property of their respomibilities. Their object was to instruct society and to leaven it with Christian principles. If the principles of the Founder of the Christ.oo faith were carried out in our political, social, and business life, English society would he revolutionised.

PREVENTING THE TRAMWAY RUSH.

The new Tramway Stiltr at tile Blackfriars e d of the Embankment is now in use. It has been ereetrd not only to give shell.' from the rain to the thousands who join the L.C.C. cars at that point, tot in order to control the crowds who rash for seats every evening. It is ornamentally luolt. The roof is of glus, and at night is well lighted by electric light. Inside It IS dIVILI:d into (oar sections, with iron railings, which will divide the traffic into four long lines and prevent clashing. Outside the entrance displayed the name of the car which is filled from that queue.
