LOCAL FLOODS.

A Chap'er of Unhappy Memories.

If the wayfarer cares to turn aside from Wilderspool Causeway to what is known as River-road, he may examine, screwed upon a cottage door post, three plates of brass which record _ _

ST. SWITHEN'S, 1828.

DECEMBER 21ST, 1837, FLOOD MARK.

THE GREAT FLOOD, 17TH NOVEMBER, 1866.

The plates form an unusual but effective flood-meter, and, polished by successive tenants (all too diligently polished, for one inscription is now quite illegible) they record the heights to which the waters rose on notable and unhappy occasions in the I past' . _ . . . .

Only three great floods are recorded here, but a complete flood-meter of Warrington would be an elaborate record, and would con- tain many markings. Indeed from time immemorial the Mersey has followed the bad habit of overflowing its banks, and many good etymologists think that its very name describes the marshy ground which its spreading waters had made.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
A SEVENTEENTH CENTURY RMFERENCE.

The earliest mention of a Mersey flood is in the rollicking poetry of Braithwaite,hetter known as "Drunken Barnaby," who was hindered here on a journey south, and gives us to understand that he compensated for the inconvenience of an outward flood by the comforts of an e. inward on _ _ _ _

Thence to Warrington, Banks oerflowed, Travellers to th Town were rowed ; Where supposing it much better. To be drown'd on hand than Water: Sweetly, neatly, I sojourned Till that deluge thence returned. This chance allusion is the only record of the flood but Barnaby was certainly not the only traveller of past days who remained in Warrington against his wish.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
EIGHTEENTH CENTURY CALAMITIES.

Of another early flood there is a brief notice in Dobson's Diary under date 26th of November 1728; " The great flood on Wed- I nesday has done great damage all over the country. At Warrington Causeway twelve people were drowned. At the Weir mill a poor boy was taken down." And Byron's " Remains " has also the following: 'ln December of the next year nineteen people were d rowned at W a rrington." This calamity may,of course,have been due Ito some cause other than a flood.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
NINETEENTH CENTURY FLOODS.

The events of the last century have received more careful notice and some few facts, at least, may be gathered concerning the distressing results of the inundations. On Friday July 25th 1828 the river rose in an alarming manner and its waters reached the highest level of which we have any record. In was the season of hay harvest and crops lay ready to be gathered. In a few hours the low lying lands were con- verted into a vast inland lake and the corn as well as the hay harvest was completely lost.

Though the farmers were the chiefl sufferers the townsfolks had severe losses to face. In days when the cellar was the domestic storeplace a flood which devastated the cellars carried loss to almost every home. On this occasion Church-street and Mersey- street had become two imposing canals and the inhabitants travelled in true Venetian fashion, (though not in the prettiest of gondolas), upon their business.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRIVATIONS OF A BISHOP.

It happened to be the date fixed for the visit of the Bishop of Chester to the Parish Church, and in due course the prelate arrived by chaise. But no conveyance could stem such a flood and the distinguished visitors were obliged to commit themselves to boats and thus reach the Church. Another experience the flood forged upon the Bishop (let us hope it was not an unusual one) was that of complete total abstinence. The rector had a well-stocked cellar like all good ecclesiastics of the period, but not the bravest of the company could be expected to dive fathoms deep to bring up the bottles. The chief external damage seems to have been done at Latchford and to Wilderspool Causeway. As the waters retreated they tore up Knutsford-road and left the materials in heaps. A complete re-making of the Causeway had to be entered upon before traffic was possible, and the damage to, property was so extensive as to make the flood an epxh-marking event. Stories are told of travellers and visitors who found immense fun in being innocently told by the natives that certain things were "there before the fit od " and certain houses built "just after."

'But to the inhabitants the subject was too grim for humour. The flood of December 21st, 1837 was a notable one and rose to a point only sir or seven inches lower than that of nine years before. Happily it fell in the winter season and there were no growing crops to suffer, but coming at the Christmas season the incursion of mud into countless homes and the whole- sale drowning of live stock was painful to a fine degree. Twenty-five years passed without serious overflow of the river. Then in January 1863 a high tide,intensified by a strong west wind, covered the town and meadows. But the submergence was brief and the damage was not great.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GREAT FLOOD.

road

The submergence known as " the great flood" occurred in November, 1866. The river, swollen by heavy rains, bore for some days a threatening and ugly appearance, and on the 15th of the month began to over- flow its banks. At ten o'clock in the morn- ing work had to be suspended at all the mills and workshops on the Latchford side, and the riverside inhabitants were driven for refuge to their bedrooms. Boats again plied in Church-street,Dial-street, Mersey-street and a portion of Bridge-street. For those who had left home before the flood the only way home was by gondola and a not very dignified climb through the upper window. Such imprisoned ones were provisioned from boats and were left to beguile the heavy hours as best they could until the waters subsided. On this occasion grave fears were enter- tained for the safety of Warriugton Bridge, the swift current having risen to within two feet of the centre arch of the bridge, but it stood firm and was not perceptibly worse for the strain placed upon it. . . . . .

Manchester shared the inconvenience of this flood and omnibuses carried passengers through the water, some of them, the news- paper declared, having only saved their feet from a wetting by putting them through the window'

On the morning of November 16th, it

seemed that the waters were lowering and the prisoners rested in patience, but the following day brought disquieting news. A telegram from Manchester stated that the water had risen six feet. Consternation reigned, until it was discovered that the message really said that the rise was six inches. poll

This was Warrington's worst flood, both in ,oint of the depth of the water and the inconvenience it caused. It is singular to note however that on the flood-gauge in River the "Great Flood" is shown as not having risen within twelve inches of the flood of 1828. That is probably due to the tact that heavy rains were the cause and the water would be higher above the bridge than below it. Once past the bridge the stream would flow rapidly seawards. The earlier floods, on the other hand, were partly tidal and would be deepest below the bridge.

Another destru'ctive flood which many ot our readers will remember occurred in July 1872. This was destructive not from reason' of its great height or magnitude but because of the crops which were uprooted and the hafstvest that was swept away. is reason to hope that the Ship Canal has deprived the river of power to thus deal out destruction, a blessing for which we surely shall not fail in thankfulness.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ANNIVERSARY.

...... A little space is left of which we may take advantage to mention an unrelated but interesting anniversary. It is just ninety years since the inhabitants of Warrington saw the town illuminated with gas..

7 The story of the efforts by which hydrogen (carburreted) gas was popularised as an illuminant is too long to tell here. London first saw it in 1806 as the result of the labours of Mr. F. A. Winser, but this was only in an experimental form. The date of the lighting of London streets was 1814, and Warrington, early among the smaller towns, had it on March 13th, 1821. It is recorded that in those days a flock of wild geese used to pass over the town in the evening in their flight from Chat Mow to their home on Cuerdley Marsh. On the evening of the illumination noises high above the town indicated that some- thing unusual was happening. Instead of passing over as usual the geese ' hovered as though bewildered, and after a seeming consultation and discussion made a slight detour over Latchford and passed to their home. A.M.

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB Nr"..•-•"..--...---%.,-,..w...--N".....-","%nj

INVENTIONS MADE BY BOYS.

I have been thinking lately that a very interesting book might be written upon the , subject of the inventions made by boys and girls. The writer of it would have to travel a long way back in history to make a begin- ning, because boys have done useful things in every age of the world-, and the story would have to be brought right up to date because they are doing good and ingenious things yet. Of course young people cannot often develop their ideas, they have not the materials and the special knowledge. But many important inventions originated at least in boyish minds. I will mention two or three which I happen to remember. I read last week of two Boooklyn school- boys—one fifteen and the other sixteen—who managed to make a wireless telegraph system of their own just by what they had read. . . . . . •

This is clever; but it is more surprising to know that Marconi himself had got the idea when he was only fourteen years old and had set up his first crude apparatus to experiment with, consisting of poles and some instru- ments made out of tin biscuit boxes. Another most important invention was that of the hydraulic crane, which was con- ceived in the mind of Lord Armstrong when he was quite a lad. Samuel Crompton began work upon his famous spinning mule when he was sixteen and he finished it before he was nineteen, This invention quite made a revolution in cotton manufacture and helped to make Lan cashire the workshop of the world. I At thirteen Eli Whitney had got the idea of the cotton gin—an invention which meant another big advance in the cotton industry. Another lad of sixteen, afterwards Sir John Brown, got the ides of the conical spring buffer for railway trucks. His plan when perfected made him both famous and rich. . . .

Samuel Crompton's idea did not bring him wealth, but it has helped to make the world rich which is a far better thing. And it must be encouraging to every boy and girl who thinks about it to remember that so many good things have started in youthful minds

Thomas Hoar, the devoted servant upon whom Gilbert White, the famous English naturalist, depended to carry out his garden plans and to look after his comfort in many ways, was noted for his exactness. On one occasion Thomas came into the room, and said : "Please, sir, I've been and broke a glass." "Broken a glass, Thomas! How did you do that ? " .

. "I'll show you, sir," he rejoined, as he 'disappeared for a moment. Returning with a glass in his hand, he let it fall on the floor, remarking : "That's how I broke it, sir !."

"There, go along, Thomas ! You are a great fool !" said his master; adding to him- self: "And I was as great a one for asking such a question!"

===== 0002601_19110318_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Swam, 111. P. ARE I N DUSTRI AL ACCIDENTS INCREASING?

Two years ago the then Home Secretary I appointed a Committee to enquire into the question of accidents in factories. In the three years preceding the appointment of the Committee there had been an alarming increase in the number of reported accidents, and it was considered advisable to enquire into the causes of this increase. In the year 1905 there were 100,609 accidents reported from places under the Factory and Work- shops Act, and in 1907 the number had risen to 124,325. The increase of non-fatal accidents in textile factories had been very alarming, having in two years risen by no less .than 44'62 per cent.! .. .

This Commrt-tee- has now presented its Report, and it is a document of very great interest and importance. The members appear to have done their work with thoroughness, and their suggestions and recommendations are extremely valuable. The Committee were unable to arrive at a very definite conclusion as to whether the great increase in the number of minor accidents are due to an actual increase or to a more complete system of notification. They felt inclined, however, to conclude that the latter suggestion was probably an explana- tion of a considerable proportion of the increase. Stricter notification cannot alto- gether account for the enormous increase of non-fatal accidents, which have risen from 78,000 in 1900, to 116,500 in 1909. It is suggested that the passing of the Workmen's Compensation Act in 1906 has tended to ensure the more complete reporting of minor accidents. This is no doubt true, but that does not explain the fact that in the three years before the Compensation Act came into law the number of reported accidents

increased by more than in the three years since.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
CAUSES OF THE INCREASE.

Making every allowance for the fuller reporting of accidents in recent years the fact remains that there has been an actual increase in the danger to life and limb in factories and workshops. A number of causes are suggested to account for this increase of minor accidents. There is first of all the question of "speeding up" of machinery. On this point there is a conflict of evidence between the employers and the workpeople. The former maintain that, in 1 the textile trades at any rate, machines run more slowly than formerly,on the other hand, the workpeople maintain that the machines tre now running faster than ever. Piece work and the bonus system were held, by some witnesses to be partly responsible for the increase in the number of accidents. The practice of "driving," which obtains in some of the Lancashire weaving sheds, was found by the Committee to be a constant source of complaint, and was generally spoken of by witnesses as tending to increase the accident risk.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO REDUCE ACCIDENTS.

The Committee make a good many tug- gestions as to what in their opinion might be done to lessen the risk of accident. They suggest, in the first place, that the most cordial relations should be encouraged between employers, workpeople, and factory inspectors, with a view of co-operation to do all possible to improve matters. Under the present system there is often a delay of a couple of years in carrying out the recom- mendations of the inspectors as to the guarding of a dangerous part of a machine. Employers differ very much in regard to the anxiety they show to safeguard life and limb. There are many employers who never require to be notified to fence machinery, while others Will wait until the visit of the inspector before doing anything to guard an obviously dangerous part. To bring about . • .

the more rapid- and general enforcement of safeguards it is suggested that there should be conferences held at reasonable intervals between the employers and the workpeople for the purpose of discussing what may be done to ensure the maximum amount of pro- tection. It is also recommended that certain factory inspectors might be asked to specialise on this question, so that their knowledge may be available for the whole staff, not all of whom have the time to become fully acquainted by their own investigations with the dangers of every machine in their dis- tricts. The crowding of machines so closely together is put down as one of the causes of I accidents, and attention is particularly drawn to insufficient space between the looms in cotton factories. More attention it is recom- mended should be paid to keeping the floors of factories clean ; lighting is often insufficient and young persons are often put to do heavy work for which they are quite unfitted. CLEANING MACHINERY.

Naturally the Committee give much prominence to the question of cleaning machinery while in motion. This is a very prolific cause of accidents, In 1909 there were no leas than 132 accidents to children under 14 in the Lancashire cotton factories from this one cause. It is recommended that children under 14 should be absolutely pro- hibited from cleaning any part of machinery which is in motion,and the prohibition should apply to persons under 18, except where the Home office gives a special exemption in reg..rd to somq particular machine. This part of the Report, is I think, the weakest. The recommendations scarcely go beyond approving the existing regulations as sufficient. The Report makes no recom- mendation for increased protection for adult women in this matter but even approves that they should be allowed to clean machinery in motion, and that they should only be prohibited by a special regulation of the Home Office applying to some special machinery. The Committee also think that it is unnecessary to suggest that a stricter interpretation should be given to the words "while the machinery is in motion." It is to be regretted that the employers' views on these matters should have so strongly influenced the recommends- tior.s of the Committee.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
FACTORY INSPECTORS.

Gill and

One of the moot valuable features of the Reportis that dealing with the qualifications of the rectory Inspectors. On this question, the Co,nmittee have not been able to agree, and there are three separate memoranda on the matter. The majority of the Committee recommend the abolition of the two grades of inspectors, and suggest that all should enter by the same examination, but that promotion to the more responsible positions snould afterwards be made on the ground of proved ability and capacity. Ot,her members of the Committee would keep the present diatinction and have an inferior class of assistant inspectors, as at present, to

ild—the more routine work of inspection. They would, however, make it possible for an alisiutant inspector of exceptional ability to receive promotion to the higher rank. Mr.

note of theil

that the el

might be donil high scholastic

practical knowle work might be d of experts.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING COMP'

A suggestion is added to the Report to the effect that there is not that communication between the workmen's organisations and the factory inspectors which ought to exist. Trade unions are specially appealed to to communicate freely with the inspectors in regard to all matters which they think require attention. Complaints from work- men's organisations invariably receive the attention of the inspectors, and their work is greatly assisted by information from those who are in daily touch with the life of the factory and the workshop. There is a strong disinclination on the part of workpeople to send reports to the inspectors, because it is often not difficulty for the employer to trace the source of the complaint; and it is some- times to the after detriment of the workman. Complaints of a general character may be safely reported to the inspector, but those which affect particular workmen only are more difficult to report with impunity. The recommendations of the Committee are almost wholly of a character whiclt could be carried out by Home Office admin- istration. At present the Home Office is engaged in framing regulations for the better protection of life in the mines. This is important and much needed work, but when that has been done attention might be given to the report of this Committee. Although the percentage of accidents is not so high in factories and workships as in the mines, yet the total number of accidents—fatal and non- fatal—is in each case about the aame. We cannot contemplate with satisfaction a state of things which involves the loss of about 1,100 lives and the maiming of 124,000 persons every year. The investigations of the Committee lead to the conclusion that much might be done to lesson this enormous number of ac^idents.

===== 0002601_19110318_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====

WIDNES VINDICATED. DEATH-RATE BELOW THE AVERAGE.

On Saturday members of the North-western centre of the Sanitary Inspectors' Asa.lation, to the number of 130, visited Widnes. All the principal towns of I,ancaehire were represented. The party Inspected the 'transporter Bridge, the two local hospitals, the water- works, and the Technical Schools, and were afterwards entertained.

Its position on the Mersey made it the centre of the astounding cheroical licom of forty years ego, and its rateable vnluo in forty years went up !torn .C 30,564 to £171,241. The effect of the sanitary and health work done since the incorpora- tion of the borough Was seen in the comparison afforded by the vital statistics. From 1097 to 1,31 the average death.iiate 1,00 was 10-3. For the last five years it w, 14.94 per IMO. The death-rote net year watt 12.4, against 13.4 for England. Who I would now say Widnes wee a had piece to live in ? Considesing the rapid growth of the place and what the municipality had done to benefit the people. the town's , financial position was eminently sound. In 11,91 the loans were £256,937, and today, despite the Council's ninny enterprises, they were only £294.9t0. And whet of the future ? Mr. Poyntz also- e-ta- ted that National service me Manhood Suffrage. Theo why does it not appear in their programme.? Also the security of the worker from want. Our Local Parliament and Protection. Sir,—ln Mr. Furnestes speech on Tariffs in the emirse of the debate in our local Parliament, I notice be makes a point of 'Food prices rising under Free Trade" Quite right, IRA I give Mr. Furnena credit for sufficient intelligence to know some of the condi- tions which prevail (and which cause rises in the prices of foodstuffs/ and the absolute independence of such rises from any Free 'frade cause. A further point is that lie gives certain comparative rises on to foodstuffs, here and on the continent. I should like to .k two questions. First, may I enquire whether we should not be worse off when all food was taxed by a '' scientific " tariff, than we ore when only natural causes affect the prices ? Second, in it not a fact that in all tariff ridden countri., they pay more than we do, arti..le for article and of equal grade? As to Mr. England and unemployment, this is a very large question but if we secure an ill-paid industry like iron, and lose a highly paid industry like steel, by reaeon of Tariff walls, are we better off? • Might I also mention, that in a debate last year in the .. • German Reichstag, it was stated that there were • • " 120,10) German workuien lout of works) over and shone the usual number at the time of year. ' unemployed." Ur. Carl Legior, president of the Elerman Federation if Trade Unions, writing to Mr. J. Ross Clyne,- of Manchester, stated that over 50,10) '- unemployed workmen in Berlin alone were known to . the unions, and they had live applicatioos for every job requiring skilled labour, and more than double that number, for jobs requiring unskilled labour.— Yours truly

The Church of England and Prayers for the Dead.

Sir,—l again crave your courtesy, deeming it very desirable that the criticisms of Layman " be dealt with. In my former letter I aaserted that prayers for the dead are :—(1) Rejected by the Prayer Book. (2/ Condemned by toe Homilies. (3) Not to be found mentioned in lioly Scripture. If I can prove those three points, then I justify my contention. First, then, in the Burial Service the Protestant position of our Prayer Book is very marked, yet, of all the service', this service presents the easiest opportunity for introducing the teaching of prayers for the dead, and this we find was the case in the pre. Reformation service of the En gliah Church. It contained false di otrine sad elaborate ceremonial from beginning to end : prayer was offered for the soul of the departed, and for the pardon of his sins. But in the First Prayer Book of Edward there is a welcome change ; ceremonialism is discarded, and the greater pert of the service is Scriptural and intelligible. At S. ' . . the same' time there were blemisbes, and one especially •• was most noticeable—the prayers were for the dead as well as for the living. In the Prayer Book of 1552 .. everything is removed from the borial service that . could countenance superstition. It totally omits all prayers for the dead ; the w .. hole .nice is intended for the livid, sot for the dead. Prayer is made that the bystapders, may be raised from the death of • eta, and aecepMel at last by the Soo of God Than does the Church of England emphasise that life is the only opportunity for conversion, and that prayers for . the dead are worthless and unscriptural. Second, prayer, for the dead are condemned by the 1,.. Homilies. A quotation will afford ample proof. "Scripture tioth acknowledge but two places after '4* ", , this life, the one proper to the elect and blessed of , Cod, the other to the reprobate and damned souls. Xherefore let WV not deceive ,ouraelves, thinking that ~.either we may help others, or r there may help us by 7 sek.„46tir good-and charitable prayers in time to come .... -,) ...., ' . . . . every incrtal dieth either in a state of ... ~ , .. , 1.. auvinen or damnation. Where then shall our prayer. ~ help and profit the dead? Be that cannot Ire saved by faith in Christ', blood, how ahall he look to be - - delivered by man's intercessions ?" Third, there is no scriptural 4'sirP port for prayers eh, for the dead. la connectimi with this, " Layman " ~. says .—" It ia important to note that proven 4 for the dead were regularly used by the Jews in their public serviees, and our Lord who attended those services in no way rebuked [ the practice." We know that the Jewish service is liturgical, and that the most solemn part of their prayers are those called "The Eighteen Prayers these the Jest; tell on were composed and instituted by Ezra. Certain it is that these prayers are very ancient, and moat of them, if not all. were used in our Saviont'a time. But though they contain many petitions for the living, they do not contain one single petition f, the dead. Can " Layman " prove that our Lord listened without demur to prayera for the dead ? I think not ! At any rate we do know that Holy Scripture contain, not one single word in favour of the practice. " The Lord grant that lie may find mercy of the Lord in that day "ace words spoken of !.... (at the time) a living, not a dead man. and conatitute no argument whatever. Dr. Puny may have been a fine writer, so also was Dr. Newman Ibefore, and after, he weeded to Rome, but that li no

:4

• miens why we should accept their teaching. Neither were the views of the late Archbishop Temple necessarily in accordance with the epirit and teaching of the Prayer Book. With regard to the testimony of ancient writers,' do not deny that prayers for the dead commenced to be offered as early as the begivning of the third century,butlaffirrn that it was a miechievons practice and one riot founded on the Word of God, which is a much safer guide than the early tradition of the Church. Finally, without further comment 1 niaint•in most emphatically that the Reformers did condemn prayers for the dead, as being contrary to

Le doctrines of the Reformed Church of Erg lend.—l am, Sir, Your obedient servant, iJAYE,

Emmanuel Chita, Reformed Episcopal Church, Bold-street. The Rector and Rome.

----- Sir.—The Rector of Warrington, besides reply- ing to what I said on Sunday, February 2oto. brings up a few other side *use. I may as well say at once that I have no intention after this of following him should other issues be brought up, as volumes could be written on the points mentioned. But I will try to be as brief as 1109- Bible here.

LETTER OF .4T. CLEMENT. In this letter of St. Clement to the Corin. thians there to no evidence of absolute supremacy, but as Chapman rays. There is in it a tone of authority," and adds ''it is truly extraordinary to rind a Bishop of Horne in the life time of the Apostle St. John writing as a superior to a Greek Church of Apostolic foundation"—nor do we read M any protest. Bishop Lightfoot speaks of it as "almost imperious" in its tone. Clement," as Spencer Jones says "evidently had the Papal con- sciousness fully developed. for lie passes over the head of St. John, and reaches out the exercise of Roman supervision to the far-off Corin- thians: nor does he mince matters in the least." These are his words: he claims " obedience to the things written by us through the Holy Spirit" (See. 63.) And a few sei.tences before making this claim he says: "If any disobey the things spoken by Him through U 3, let them know that they will involve them selves in transgression and no small peril." It is no wonder then that Lightfoot ehould charac- terise this letter as "the first step towards Papal • Di mination." That is to say (lenient had the ' " Papal Consciousness" of universal jurisdiction. 0.0 96. It would seem that Clement's letter far • from telling against Papal Supremacy tells for it. _

ATIL..INA-SIAN CREED.

In reply to the Hector's aspersions on 1 my historical knowledge as to this Creed, *WI, that I ne‘er .'ai'l St. Athanashm ' was the author in its present font,. I am quite aware this cannot be proved. Some assert that the Saint composed it during his exile. and in this, as an tiie,rian says, " there is nothing intrinsically improbable, only it lacks confirmation." Another historian, Waterland, ascribes it in its present form to St. Hilary 14301: , another. Morin, to casarius of Arles; another to Virgilius of Thapsue in the Fifth Century. This Ipoint may never be satisfactorily cleared up. But .as the historian Brewer 118721 says, "Whether , written by Athanasius or not, its teaching is dis- tinctly ,Athanasian. But why should Canon Willis cavil at its teaching, viz., that the " Holy Ghost pniceede from the Father and the Son," when lie subscribed to it as a clergyman, and when. I presume, he still recites it as ordered at stated times of the year? V.

?11CT0C910.5.14. ETC.

The Rector challe Tea rue to quote any Greek Father alto uses the word " afetousiosig," or a Latin Father the word "Transubstantiation." before the Eleventh Century. I never asserted, nor do I now assert, that any Father aged these very words before that elate; hut I did assert. and do now, that, they did use their equivalent. i.e., a 'real" chany of "substance" at con- secration, which these words mean.

THE NATURE OF THE CHANGE. , The change consists in this: That before the words of consecration there is simply Bread and Wine, after the words, the substance of Bread I end Wine no longer remain, but are changed into the substance of the Body and Blood of Christ, but that the appearance of Bread. etc.. i.e.. species mr accidents, still remain not adhering by the power of God to any substance. And this lir:ngs us to the words attributed to:

POPE GELASIES. "the elements abide in their own proper nature" In the first place there can he little doubt that the work from which these words are taken was not the work of the great Pope of that : name. They have probably been taken front some collection of stork controversial passages, land have often done duty in that way since the sixteenth century. The editors of Migue, one of the greatest authorities on such points, refuse •to attribute these words to Pope Gelasius. a course which they say is justified by the "corn- tennis doctorum virorunr nostri steculi sententii;. But even if the words were used, the person who used them merely meant that the elements, or, as we sac now, the species or accidents, still • retain their former appearance. We must not. I forget that in the early centuries the echolastio terms: Substance, essence, nature, etc., had not been differentiated by tile schoohnen, and hence we cannot expect front the men of those early days the same philosophical accuracy. But of this we are quite sure that front the very beginning, both East and West have ever held that after consecration there is a real change of substance, and this it is that matters. Will the Rector please cite that out of the eleven historians I quote, eight are Protestants. VIII.

Mr. Philip Snowden, M.P., and the Osborne Judgment.

Sir,—Baoing read Mr. P. Snowden's reference to the Osborne judgment in your issue of last Saturday, will you allow me to state that because a great number of people are practically nmpelled to join trade unions in order to pursue in peace the occupa- tions by which they live, it is therefore undesirable to allow their funds to be used for political purposes ; for if it were no. then the trade unions would become also political societies. and many in order to obtain a living would have to support what they believe to lie injurious to the common weal. We ought not to allow either man r r woman to be coerced to join any polit.cal party, because every citizen ought to be at liberty to use his money and influence in the way he thinks best for the country, and it is the duty of the Government to protect them in doing no. If the 'Government disregard this duty, it may lead to the beginning of the downfall of the British Empire, and the beginning of a coming slavery. Mr. Snowden says, "he has found a number of MP's who have given written pledgee Insole against their party if necessary, because they found oat at the recent election that this in esti on was going to settle whether they were retained or not." It may not be of much importance whether or not a particular individual is returned, but the welfare of the country is of the greatest importance. It may be very little that we can do to promote it, but the object is so infinitely great that it is worth our while to do that little. It is better to do that little than to dwell "in the tabernacles of the wicked." John Stuart Mill and John Bright were strongly opposed to members of Parliament being paid by the State and I think diet- would have objected to the Osborne judgment being re‘ersed.— Yours, &co S. KENYON. Itibbleton, March 11th, 1911.

MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P. Correspondence With Mr. Handel Booth, MP.

To TIM Et,ll,Plt,

Sir,—l shall be glad if you will insert in your next leans the enclosed correspondence I have had with Mr. Handel Booth, M.P. . .

I think the abbreviated press report of Mr. Booth's speech on the subject of insanitary property in Warrington was somewhat mislead. Mg. and the citizens of the borough will be pleased to see that ho makes no charge whatever against our Corporation. I considered that the good name of the b 'rough was concerned, and I therefore felt it to be my I duty to write Mr. Booth. and I am pleased to have obtained his very frank disclaimer.—Yeirs faithfully, HAROLD SMITH. March It. I House of Commons Library. March 14th. Dear Mr. Boot It,— From the enclosed extract from the report of the last meeting of the Warrington Town Council you will see that noise members attribute to you and warmly repudiate a statement to the effect that members of the Council own insanitary property.

I am hy no means satisfied that such a con. struction can be placed upon your speech, and I shall, as the representative of the Borough. he very pleased if I have your authority for stating that no such charge was made or suggested.— Believe me, yours sincerely.

HAROLD SMITH.

/Mow, of Commons Library, March 14th, 1911. Dear Mr. Smith,— . .

..... , I am glad that you have drawn my attention , to die statement at the Warrington Tows Council meeting.M The ayor waa quite right in his view that I did not criticise the councillors of Warrington. Several towns occurred to my mind, hut they are not situate even in Lancashire. Anyone who heard my references to the whole question would not suggest that I wished to reflect upon your Corporation. I only wiah that snore authorities wore as thorough as yours.—Believe me, yours faithfully,

F. HANDEL BOOTH.

Mr. flobhoutte 'by written answer) : Claim, to be charged at the lower rate of income tax in respect of earned income are rittired by law :o be made by taxpayers prior to the 30th of September in the year of charge, and failure to prefer a claim thereto within the prescribed period involves a lies of the relief. Farming in Canada means 100% Annual Profit.

FREEMASONS' TRIBUTE TO THE LATE KING. One of the first s'atues to be erected to the tile King Edward is nearing o npletion. It is subscribed for by the Freemasons of the North and North-East districts of London as a tribute of affection to the memory of the late Grand Patron of the Order and its former Grand Master. The bust is to be in bronze, 2 ft. 8 in. high, just over life-size, and will stand on • pedestal of polished Peterhead granite. The late King is shown in his Coronation robes, and thc various Orders he wears arc accurately modelled. It will be erected in the Mile-end Road.
